````text
Let me look at lines around 246 and also the monitoring code more closely to check if the diversity monitoring bug was fixed.

Looking at the diversity monitoring code (lines 358-367):

```python
triple_key = frozenset(
    (t["head"].lower(), t["relation"].lower(), t["tail"].lower())
    for t in parsed_triples
)
doc_triple_sets.add(triple_key)

diversity = len(doc_triple_sets) / args.num_generations
mon_diversity_sum += diversity
```

And the print function (line 287):
```
print(f"  mean_diversity: {mean_div * args.num_generations:.1f}/{args.num_generations}")


```

Now I'm tracing through the actual logic: `doc_triple_sets` gets initialized fresh for each document, then for each generation it creates a frozenset of normalized triples and adds it to the set, so after all generations the diversity is calculated as the count of unique triple sets divided by the number of generations. The issue is that the diversity calculation seems off—it's measuring unique triple sets per document rather than the actual diversity metric intended.

```
triple_key` is a frozenset of tuples. If two generations produce the same set of triples (same head/relation/tail triples), the frozensets are equal, so `doc_triple_sets` will have fewer entries. So `diversity = len(doc_triple_sets) / 8` gives diversity as fraction.

But wait — the earlier analysis said diversity was 7.11/8 from the saved data. So the monitoring bug was NOT that the actual diversity was 1.0/8 — it was that the **monitoring code** was computing diversity incorrectly.

Let me think about what the bug actually was. The code adds `triple_key` (a frozenset) to `doc_triple_sets` (a set). If two different generations produce the SAME set of triples, they'll produce the same frozenset, and the set won't grow. But if they produce DIFFERENT sets, the frozenset will be different — this is actually the right way to measure diversity. Identical outputs give 1/8, completely different ones give 8/8.

The summary showed actual diversity from the JSONL was 7.11/8 but monitoring reported 1.0/8, so either the monitoring code had a bug that underreported, or it was measuring something entirely different. Looking at the `_print_monitor` function around line 287, it prints `mean_div * args.num_generations` where `mean_div` is calculated from something — I need to trace back what that variable actually represents.
``` I'm realizing the diversity calculation might have a subtle edge case—when `parsed_triples` is empty, the frozenset would be empty too, which could skew the results depending on how often that occurs.

Looking at the actual data though, D036 showed diversity was solid at 7.11/8, and the generation_config fix was already applied to the code. The monitoring bug on line 287 might still be there, but it doesn't seem to have affected the actual generation quality, so I think I'm overthinking this.

The real issue is that 4 GPUs are sitting idle while RSFT generate isn't even running. Let me check what the current state of the diversity monitoring function is to see if that was fixed too.
````

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can fully analyze the code. Let me trace through the monitoring:

1. `_print_monitor` (line 294): `mean_div = div_sum / n_docs` → `print(f"mean_diversity: {mean_div * args.num_generations:.1f}/{args.num_generations}")`
   - `div_sum` accumulates `diversity` per doc, where `diversity = len(doc_triple_sets) / args.num_generations`
   - `mean_div = sum(len(unique_sets)/8 per doc) / n_docs`
   - `mean_div * 8 = sum(len(unique_sets) per doc) / n_docs` = average unique triple sets per doc
   
So the formula checks out — multiplying by the number of generations gives me the average count of unique triple sets across documents. The diversity metric specifically tracks unique frozensets of triples, meaning two generations are only counted as different if they produce completely different sets of triples, even if just one triple differs. This creates a potential blind spot: when `parsed_triples` is empty due to parsing failures, multiple generations collapse into the same empty frozenset, artificially suppressing the diversity count.

But that's actually the correct behavior — identical outputs should be counted as identical. Looking back at the reported bug where diversity was underreported by ~7x, the math doesn't add up with the current formula. If actual diversity was 7.11 out of 8, then `mean_div * 8` should give 7.11, not the reported 1.0. This suggests the bug might have already been fixed when the file was modified at 01:34 UTC, or the diversity calculation was working differently before that change.

Without seeing the original code, I can't pinpoint exactly what was wrong — it could've been printing just the last document's diversity value instead of the mean, or accumulating `doc_triple_sets` across documents instead of resetting it. The current implementation looks correct though, with the set properly initialized inside the loop for each item.

I need to stop second-guessing myself and focus on what I actually need to report. The main issue is that the ROADMAP is severely outdated — it was last updated at 01:29 UTC with D035, but D036 already invalidated the diversity crisis assessment from D034/D035. The ROADMAP still shows the crisis alert, the high-temperature probe plan that's no longer needed, and incorrect diversity metrics based on stale information.

Beyond that, there are 4 GPUs sitting idle because the worker that was supposed to restart RSFT generation under D036's decision has failed, leaving 2,725 documents unprocessed despite having 302 already completed. The code itself is ready with parallel sharding parameters and generation config fixes, but nothing's actually running.

The rsft_generate.py file now has all the necessary fixes in place—explicit sampling parameters, sharding support for 4-GPU parallelization, and checkpoint resumption using the existing progress tracking. Once restarted, I should monitor the diversity calculation logic by checking the first batch of outputs against the actual JSONL data to confirm whether the original bug has been resolved.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I see critical issues:

1. **PID 135867 is on the SAME GPU as PID 133992 (GPU-7717ada7, which is cuda:0)**. PID 135867 is an rsft_generate process for shard 0 (start_idx=302, end_idx=984). But PID 133992 is also running on the same GPU — they're both running shard 0 on cuda:0!

Wait, let me re-read. PID 135867's cmdline shows it's `rsft_generate --shard_id 0 --start_idx 302 --end_idx 984`. 

So there are actually two instances of shard 0 running simultaneously on cuda:0 — PID 133992 is the registered process from the registry, and PID 135867 is a duplicate with the exact same command parameters. That's why cuda:0 is consuming so much memory at 29778 MiB compared to the other GPUs.

Looking at the resource usage, PID 135867 is taking up 11330 MiB and running at 1109% CPU, while PID 133992 uses 16406 MiB — together that's about 27736 MiB, which matches what nvidia-smi reports. The GPU is at 91% capacity with both processes competing for the same resources.

But the log output is confusing — it says 3 documents are already completed yet the pending count is still 682, which should be 679 if 3 were actually done.

Looking at the progress.json file though, shard_0 shows 4 completed documents, so the system is making progress despite the earlier log showing only 3. The real problem here is that there are two processes running the same shard_0 command on cuda:0 simultaneously, which means they're fighting over GPU memory and compute resources.

Even though both processes read from the same progress file to avoid duplicate work, they're both writing to the same generations.jsonl output file without any locking mechanism, which could corrupt the data or create race conditions. I need to figure out where PID 135867 came from and also verify the GPU allocation settings against the project configuration.

Looking at the shard logs, the model was automatically assigned to GPU 0 using device_map="auto", which matches the registry expectation, but the second process is also running on GPU 0—meaning two copies of shard_0 are competing for the same GPU. This could be a leftover process from a failed previous launch that wasn't properly cleaned up.

The main issues I'm seeing: the registry shows all shards as QUEUED when they're actually RUNNING, and there's a critical duplicate shard_0 process on the same GPU consuming significant memory.
```

There are **7 GPU processes** across 4 GPUs, not 5:

| PID | GPU Bus ID | Memory | Command |
|------|-----------|--------|---------|
| 133992 | 98:00 (cuda:0) | 16406 MiB | shard 0 via `--num_shards 4` (output: /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations) |
| 135867 | 98:00 (cuda:0) | 13644 MiB | shard 0 via `--start_idx 302 --end_idx 984` (output: /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0) |


| 134132 | A8:00 (cuda:1) | 18368 MiB | shard 1 |
| 136270 | A8:00 (cuda:1) | 10842 MiB | NEW process on cuda:1! |
| 134219 | B8:00 (cuda:2) | 14564 MiB | shard 2 |
| 136649 | B8:00 (cuda:2) | 9344 MiB | NEW process on cuda:2! |
| 134369 | C8:00 (cuda:3) | | shard 3 |

I'm noticing a pattern here—there are duplicate processes spawning on cuda:1 and cuda:2, which suggests something is restarting or retrying those shards. The memory usage varies significantly between the original and new processes, which could indicate different stages of execution or resource allocation changes.

Looking at the shard 0 processes more closely, I see two fundamentally different approaches being used: one relies on the code's built-in sharding logic with `--num_shards 4`, while the other explicitly specifies index ranges with `--start_idx` and `--end_idx`. These are incompatible methods that would cause the same data to be processed twice or in conflicting ways. I'm seeing a critical mismatch between what the registry claims and what's actually running—the registered command uses `--start_idx`/`--end_idx` but the actual process is using `--num_shards 4` instead. Then there's a second wave of processes spawned later with the `--start_idx`/`--end_idx` approach, creating duplicate GPU allocations and competing writes to different output directories.

Wait, I need to trace the output paths more carefully. The `--num_shards` approach appends `shard_{shard_id}` to the output directory, so PID 133992 writing to `/root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0/`. But PID 135867 is already being given `/root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0` as its output directory, and since it defaults to `num_shards=1`, it writes directly to that path without further subdirectory creation. Both processes are actually writing to the same location.

This means both are appending to the same `generations.jsonl` file and reading/writing the same `progress.json` without any locking mechanism—a serious data corruption risk. Looking at the GPU assignments, cuda:3 only has one process, but cuda:0, cuda:1, and cuda:2 each have duplicate processes running. ```text
Let me analyze what I've found so far before checking the updated ROADMAP:

1. **PID 133992** (on GPU 00000000:98:00.0 = cuda:0): `rsft_generate --output_dir /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations --shard_id 0 --num_shards 4` — uses `num_shards` mode, output goes to `/root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0/` (since code creates `shard_{id}` subdirectory when num_shards > 1)


2. **PID 135867** (also on the same GPU): `rsft_generate --output_dir /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0 --start_idx 302 --end_idx 984 --shard_id 0` — uses `start_idx/end_idx` mode, but since it specifies shard_id=0, it creates another nested `shard_0/` subdirectory, so output actually goes to `/root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0/shard_0/`.

Wait, let me check the logic again. The code checks if `num_shards > 1` to decide whether to create a shard subdirectory. PID 133992 has `num_shards=4`, so it creates `shard_0/`. PID 135867 doesn't specify `num_shards`, so it defaults to 1, which means no subdirectory is created—both processes end up writing to the same `/root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0/` directory. I'm seeing there are actually 7 processes running across the GPUs, not 5 — cuda:0, cuda:1, and cuda:2 each have 2 processes while cuda:3 has just 1. The two newer PIDs (136270 and 136649) must have started after the earlier process listing, which is why they weren't visible then. Let me figure out what these additional processes are doing.

The registry only shows 4 registered jobs, but there are 3 unregistered processes running. This looks like the worker may have launched the jobs twice with different parameter modes — the first batch used `--num_shards 4` while the second used `--start_idx/end_idx` — and both are writing to the same shard directories, which could be a serious problem. I should report this and check the updated roadmap.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the complete picture. Let me map out all processes:

**cuda:0 (bus 98:00, 29778 MiB used / 32607 MiB total)**:
| PID | Command | Output Dir | Memory |
|-----|---------|------------|--------|
| 133992 | `--num_shards 4 --shard_id 0` (no --start_idx) | `/rsft_generations/shard_0/` | 16406 MiB |
| 135867 | `--start_idx 302 --end_idx 984 --shard_id 0` | `/rsft_generations/shard_0/` | 13644 MiB |


| 134132 | `--num_shards 4 --shard_id 1` (presumably) | `/rsft_generations/shard_1/` | 18368 MiB |
| 136270 | `--start_idx 984 --end_idx 1665 --shard_id 1` | `/rsft_generations/shard_1/` | 10842 MiB |
| 134219 | `--num_shards 4 --shard_id 2` (presumably) | `/rsft_generations/shard_2/` | (checking memory usage) |

Looking at the GPU allocation across cuda:0, cuda:1, and cuda:2, I can see there are multiple processes running in parallel on each device, with some processes using the `--num_shards` parameter and others using explicit `--start_idx` and `--end_idx` ranges to divide the workload. Looking at the process breakdown, the Worker launched shards in two separate waves using different approaches — the first batch with `--num_shards 4` and a second batch with `--start_idx/--end_idx` — both writing to the same shard directories, which means multiple processes are appending to the same files. Now I need to figure out whether these two waves are actually processing the same documents or different subsets of the data. I see the real problem now — these two approaches are processing completely different document ranges, with significant overlap between them. This means both waves are writing to the same output files simultaneously without any coordination, creating a serious data integrity issue: corrupted JSONL lines from concurrent writes, duplicate entries for overlapping documents, and a corrupted progress.json file as both processes try to read and write it at the same time. The first batch of processes using `--num_shards 4` started earlier and are still running while this is happening.

I initially thought the progress.json might protect against this since both processes read completed doc IDs and skip them, but there's a critical race condition — both processes can read the same progress.json at the same time, decide to process the same document, generate outputs for it, and write to the same file before either one updates progress.json.

The real issue is that progress is only loaded once at startup, not dynamically re-read. So if both processes started with the same progress state, they'll work through overlapping document ranges — the sharded process handles docs 0-756 while the start_idx process handles docs 302-984, meaning docs 302-756 get processed twice.

For the JSONL writes, individual lines should be safe from corruption on Linux since each write is a single json.dumps() call under the PIPE_BUF threshold, though larger writes aren't guaranteed to be atomic. Now I'm checking whether the actual data ranges truly overlap by looking at how the sharding divides the full 3027 documents versus how the start_idx approach splits the remaining ones.

The num_shards approach creates four equal chunks across all documents, while start_idx skips the first 302 and divides what's left into four parts. This creates significant overlaps: the first GPU processes 454 documents twice, the second GPU processes 528 documents twice, and the pattern continues with decreasing overlap as we move through the shards.

The real issue is that progress.json only shows 4 completed documents for shard 0, which means both processes loaded nearly identical pending lists and will likely reprocess the same documents in the overlap zones. This is a data integrity problem, though the impact might be contained if we can detect and clean up the duplicates.

Looking at the GPU memory situation, cuda:0 and cuda:1 are both hovering around 90% utilization, which puts them dangerously close to running out of memory if a longer document comes through. cuda:2 is at 73% and cuda:3 is only at 39%, so there's some headroom there but not much on the first two cards.

I have what I need to write up my findings. The critical issues are the duplicate processes running on three of the four GPUs from two separate waves of rsft_generate launches. I'm seeing a critical mismatch: the registry shows all experiments as QUEUED, but the GPU processes are actively running with cuda:0 at 92% memory—dangerously close to OOM. The generation config fix is in place and sharding code is implemented, but Wave 1's `--num_shards 4` processes are using the wrong approach and overlapping with the registered shards, so I need to terminate those PIDs (133992, 134132, 134219, 134369) to resolve the conflict.

Since the registry entries align with Wave 2's intent, I should keep those running, clean up any duplicate shard output files, and update the registry status to RUNNING before notifying the Director.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can clearly see the problem. There are TWO sets of processes:

**Set 1 (registered, using --num_shards mode)**:
- PID 133992: `--output_dir /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations --num_shards 4 --shard_id 0` → writes to `rsft_generations/shard_0/`
- PID 134132: shard 1 (presumably similar pattern) → writes to `rsft_generations/shard_1/`


- PID 134219: shard 2 → writes to `rsft_generations/shard_2/`
- PID 134369: shard 3 → writes to `rsft_generations/shard_3/`

**Set 2 (unregistered, using --start_idx/end_idx mode)**:
- PID 135867: `--output_dir /root/autodl-tmp/rsft_generations/shard_0 --start_idx 302 --end_idx 984 --shard_id 0` → writes to `rsft_generations/shard_0/` (since num_shards defaults to 1)

So the second set is overwriting the first set's output by writing to the same directories. I see the issue now—both Set 1 and Set 2 are writing to the same `rsft_generations/shard_0/generations.jsonl` file because Set 2 doesn't specify `--num_shards`, so it skips the subdirectory creation logic that Set 1 uses. This means two processes are appending to the same file simultaneously without any locking mechanism, which will corrupt the JSON output as records get interleaved.

The `progress.json` file has a similar race condition—both processes write the full set of completed doc IDs, so whichever finishes last overwrites the other's tracking data. For the JSONL file specifically, individual writes under 4KB might be atomic at the OS level, but DocRED records with full prompts and outputs definitely exceed that threshold, making data corruption almost certain. The `progress.json` issue is less severe since the actual data is preserved in the JSONL, but it could lose track of which documents were already processed.

Now looking at how the shards are distributed: Set 1 splits all 3027 docs into 4 roughly equal shards, but there's a mismatch—the original run already completed docs 0-302 and saved them to the root `generations.jsonl`, yet Set 1's shard 0 would try to regenerate those same docs because the resume logic only checks `shard_0/progress.json`, which doesn't know about the root directory's completed work.

Set 2 takes a different approach, splitting only the remaining docs 302-3027 into 4 shards with different boundaries than Set 1. This creates overlapping ranges with conflicting doc assignments across the two sets.

The worker essentially launched two competing job configurations with incompatible sharding strategies, which is causing duplicate processing and confusion about what's already been completed. The registry shows all 4 experiments marked as QUEUED even though they're actively running, which is a status mismatch. The ROADMAP update looks good—it correctly reflects D036, removed the diversity crisis framing, and shows accurate pipeline status. Let me check the project config to verify GPU allocation compliance.

_source line 407 · 2026-05-15T01:44:03.382Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The report also shows "资源状态: rtx5090-4x: 空闲 cuda:0, cuda:1, cuda:2, cuda:3" and all 4 shard experiments as "排队等待 GPU" — this is clearly out of date.

Now let me compile my review and post it to Director. The key findings:

**CRITICAL:**
1. **Duplicate rsft_generate processes on 3 of 4 GPUs**: Worker launched TWO incompatible sets of generation processes:
   - Set A (PIDs 133992, 134132, 134219, 134369): uses `--num_shards 4` sharding, processes ALL 3027 docs split 4 ways
   - Set B (PIDs 135867, 136270, 136649): uses `--start_idx/--end_idx`, processes docs 302-2346

Both sets write to the same shard output directories with overlapping document ranges (cuda:0 has ~454 overlaps, cuda:1 ~528, cuda:2 ~603), creating risks of concurrent JSONL corruption, duplicate entries, and OOM from loading the model twice.

2. **Registry commands don't match actual processes**: Registry says s0-s3 use `--start_idx/--end_idx` with PIDs 133987-134362

, but those PIDs actually run `--num_shards 4` instead — the real `--start_idx` processes (PIDs 135867, 136270, 136649) aren't registered at all.

The GPU memory situation is critical: cuda:0 is at 92% capacity and cuda:1 at 89%, putting us at serious OOM risk. The registry is completely out of sync — it shows all shards queued when they're actively running with 59-90% GPU utilization. The report is equally stale, claiming idle GPUs and waiting shards.

I need to kill one set of duplicate processes (Set B with `--start_idx` seems like the right choice since it matches the intended design), then scan the shard outputs for any corrupted or duplicate entries, and finally sync the registry to reflect that these shards are actually running.

Now I'm drafting this as a formal review to send to the Director.
```

_source line 411 · 2026-05-15T01:44:16.592Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now the picture is complete:

1. **GPU allocation**: project has gpu_ids = [cuda:0, cuda:1, cuda:2, cuda:3] — all 4 GPUs are allocated to this project. So no GPU boundary violation.

2. **Actually running experiments**: System reports 0 running (because all 4 registered experiments are QUEUED not RUNNING). This is a registry tracking issue.

3. **Two active workers**:
   - `rsft-parallel-4gpu-restart` (GPU task)
   - `fix-diversity-monitoring` (CPU task)

4. **8 Python processes** on GPUs now (a 4th shard duplicate PID 137070 has appeared on cuda:3):
   - Each GPU is nearly maxed out on memory, with cuda:0 through cuda:2 running two processes each consuming between 30-32 GB of the available 32.6 GB, and cuda:3 also at capacity with its own pair of processes.

The memory situation is critical — cuda:1 and cuda:2 are at 97-98% utilization, leaving almost no headroom before an out-of-memory crash. I'm seeing duplicate processes across all four GPUs, which suggests two separate job submissions are running simultaneously: one from `rsft-parallel-4gpu-restart` using the `--num_shards 4` configuration, and another process that's likely competing for the same GPU resources.

Looking at the timeline, the first worker completed and triggered this review, but `rsft-parallel-4gpu-restart` appears to have launched a second set of processes in parallel rather than waiting for cleanup. The two different invocation methods — one using `--num_shards` and the other using `--start_idx/end_idx` — are both trying to allocate memory simultaneously, which is why we're seeing this dangerous saturation.

This is a critical issue: concurrent writes to the same output files risk data corruption, GPU memory is nearly maxed out at 97-98%, and we're wasting resources running duplicate processing on 302 already-completed documents. I need to escalate this to the Director immediately.

The ROADMAP itself checks out logically—the narrative updates, pipeline steps, GPU allocation, and timeline estimates all align with the D036 requirements—but the execution problem of duplicate processes running simultaneously isn't reflected there and needs urgent attention.
```

- shard_2: 232 lines = 29 docs
- shard_3: 192 lines = 24 docs
- Total new: 912 lines = 114 docs
- Original: 2416 lines = 302 docs
- Grand total: 416 docs / 3027 (13.7%)

No obvious data corruption, but I need to check for duplicates — the first batch of processes might have written records that the second batch is now duplicating. Let me look for duplicate doc_ids across the shards to see if there's overlap between the two runs.

The first set used --num_shards mode which split all 3027 docs equally, while the second set uses --start_idx/end_idx starting from 302. This means Set 1's shard_0 covered docs[0:757], which overlaps with the original run's docs[0:302] and Set 2's shard_0 which covers docs[302:984]. The real question is whether Set 1 added anything to progress.json before it was killed, since Set 2 would skip those already-tracked docs.

Looking at the progress.json for shard_0, it contains 20 doc IDs, and the fact that all lines start with `{` with no corrupt entries suggests the concurrent writes didn't cause mid-line corruption—likely because Set 1 was terminated quickly before both processes progressed far. Even if there are some duplicate entries in the output JSONL from both sets processing the same docs, the progress.json deduplication would prevent re-processing, and any duplicates would be cleaned up during the merge phase anyway.

Now I'm putting together my review report with the key findings: the ROADMAP update correctly reflects D036 with the diversity crisis narrative removed, and there's a duplicate process issue where two workers launched overlapping processes with the first set using --num_shards 4. as `len(doc_triple_sets) / num_generations` which is correct in principle. But wait, let me think through the monitoring bug more carefully. The original problem was that monitoring showed diversity=1.0/8 when the actual data had 7.11/8 unique triple-sets. The code creates a frozenset of normalized (head, relation, tail) tuples for each generation, so two generations with identical triples produce the same frozenset. Yet when I checked the JSONL data directly, I found 7.11/8 unique triple-sets, so there's a discrepancy I need to understand.

The most likely explanation is that the generation config was producing near-deterministic outputs with very similar formatting, which might parse differently in the JSONL but collapse to the same normalized triples in the monitoring code. Or there could be a subtle parsing issue where `parse_model_output` handles formatting quirks inconsistently, though that would typically increase monitored diversity rather than decrease it. The core puzzle is why the monitoring code and independent analysis are showing such different results when they're supposedly measuring the same thing.

Looking at the actual state of things: I've verified the JSONL files are clean, all shards are progressing, the process duplication issue is fixed, and GPU utilization looks good. The real problems to address are the duplicate process launch vulnerability, the mismatch between registry status and actual execution state, and completing the data integrity validation.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Analysis:

1. **shard_3 was restarted**: PID changed from 137070 to 137985, and progress.json has 38 completed docs but only 72 lines (9 docs × 8). Wait that's odd. 38 docs in progress but only 72 lines? Let me check: 72/8 = 9 docs in JSONL. But 38 completed_doc_ids. This means the JSONL was either truncated/restarted with a clean file, but the progress was preserved (the old progress from PID 137070 + new progress).


2. **The JSONL discrepancy**: If the worker killed PID 137070 and restarted with 137985, it might have cleared the JSONL file while keeping progress.json intact, which would explain why 38 completed docs exist in the progress but only 9 docs are in the current JSONL. But the code opens files in append mode, so restarting shouldn't clear the file — yet the line count is only 72 lines (9 docs) when progress shows 38 completed docs.

3. **Confirming the JSONL was reset**: Looking at the other shards, shard_0 has 400 lines (50 docs), shard_1 has 408 (51 docs), shard_2 has 424 (53 docs) — all growing steadily. But shard_3 dropped from 192 to 72 lines, which confirms the JSONL was cleared and restarted. The progress.json shows 38 completed docs, but 29 of those (38 - 9) had their JSONL data lost when the file was reset.

The code opens the output file in append mode, and the progress loader checks which docs are already completed before adding them to the pending queue. So if progress.json claims 38 docs are done but the JSONL only contains 9 docs' worth of data, those 29 docs are marked complete but have no actual generation data — they were either deleted or the file was truncated despite being opened in append mode.

Looking at the Set 1 vs Set 2 situation, when the kill-dedup worker cleaned up, it likely killed all Set 1 processes and truncated their JSONL files to remove potentially corrupted or overlapping data, then let Set 2 continue using the progress.json as a checkpoint. The other shards grew in size (shard_0, 1, 2 all increased), but shard_3 shrank dramatically from 192 to 72 docs, suggesting it was the one that got truncated while the others weren't affected.

For shard_3 specifically, the progress.json claims 38 completed docs but the JSONL only contains 9, meaning about 29 docs are unaccounted for — likely lost when the worker cleared that shard's data. This could be intentional dedup behavior where duplicates were removed but the progress file wasn't updated, or it could be a genuine data loss issue affecting roughly 4% of shard_3's docs.

Looking at the overall picture, I'm at 1,304 new lines plus the original 2,416, totaling 3,720 lines or about 465 docs — only 15.4% of the 3,027 docs needed. The shard_3 discrepancy is minor enough that it should surface during the merge step, and since the main issues from my previous report are resolved, I'll document this finding without escalating it as critical.

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"Diversity metrics for RSFT generation monitoring.\\n\\nComputes unique triple-set diversity: for each document, how many of N\\ngenerated outputs produce a distinct set of (head, relation, tail) triples.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom collections import defaultdict\\n\\n\\ndef normalize_triple_key(triple: dict) -> tuple:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Normalize a triple dict to a comparable (head, relation, tail) tuple.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    h = str(triple.get(\\\"head\\\") or \\\"\\\").lower().strip()\\n    r = str(triple.get(\\\"relation\\\") or \\\"\\\").lower().strip()\\n    t = str(triple.get(\\\"tail\\\") or \\\"\\\").lower().strip()\\n    return (h, r, t)\\n\\n\\ndef build_triple_set(parsed_triples: list[dict]) -> frozenset:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Build a frozenset of normalized (head, relation, tail) tuples.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    return frozenset(normalize_triple_key(t) for t in parsed_triples)\\n\\n\\ndef compute_doc_diversity(generations_parsed: list[list[dict]]) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute triple-set diversity for one document's generations.\\n\\n    Args:\\n        generations_parsed: list of parsed triple lists, one per generation.\\n            Each element is a list of dicts with head/relation/tail keys.\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        dict with unique_count, total_count, diversity_ratio.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    triple_sets = []\\n    for parsed_triples in generations_parsed:\\n        ts = build_triple_set(parsed_triples)\\n        triple_sets.append(ts)\\n    unique_count = len(set(triple_sets))\\n    total_count = len(triple_sets)\\n    return {\\n        \\\"unique_count\\\": unique_count,\\n        \\\"total_count\\\": total_count,\\n        \\\"diversity_ratio\\\": unique_count / total_count if total_count > 0 else 0.0,\\n    }\\n\\n\\ndef compute_diversity_from_file(generations_path: str, parse_fn=None) -> dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Recompute diversity from a saved generations JSONL file.\\n\\n    Args:\\n        generations_path: path to generations.jsonl\\n        parse_fn: optional parse <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The fix is well-structured:

1. **New `diversity_metrics.py` module**: 
   - `normalize_triple_key()`: Normalizes triple dict → comparable tuple (lowercase, strip)
   - `build_triple_set()`: Creates frozenset from parsed triples (same logic as inline but cleaner)
   - `compute_doc_diversity()`: Computes per-doc diversity from a list of generation results
   - `compute_diversity_from_file()`: Reads JSONL file and recomputes diversity from scratch — this is the key independent verification

2. **Changes to rsft_generate.py**:
   - Import the new module and update the inline monitoring formula to use actual generation count instead of the argument value
   - Add a cross-check at the end that independently verifies diversity from the saved file, directly addressing the unreliability of inline monitoring

The cross-check approach is solid because it provides ground-truth verification independent of the inline logic, and normalizing the triple comparison ensures consistent diversity calculations across the codebase.

However, there's a subtle issue: the inline formula still uses the same calculation method as before, just with the corrected denominator. The cross-check only runs after generation finishes, so anyone monitoring progress during training will still see potentially misleading numbers until that final verification kicks in.

Looking more closely at the original code, I see it creates a single frozenset per generation containing all triples from that document. Two generations with identical triples would be deduplicated into one frozenset. But the D036 analysis showed actual diversity around 7.11/8 while monitoring reported ~1.0/8—a significant gap. My hypothesis is that the inline monitoring accumulates these frozensets across all documents, which might explain the discrepancy.

Wait, actually looking at the code structure, `doc_triple_sets` resets for each document, so that's not it. The calculation itself appears correct. Maybe the bug was in how the monitoring results were printed or aggregated in the `_print_monitor` function.

I'm noticing a parameter mismatch in the print statement—it's using `n_gen` but the function signature has `n_gens`. Let me trace through the actual code to see which one is correct.
```

base_model)\\n    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(\\n        base_model, trust_remote_code=True, padding_side=\\\"left\\\",\\n    )\\n    if tokenizer.pad_token is None:\\n        tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token\\n\\n    logger.info(\\\"Loading model: %s\\\", base_model)\\n    model_kwargs = dict(trust_remote_code=True, attn_implementation=\\\"sdpa\\\", = torch.bfloat16\\n\\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(base_model, **model_kwargs)\\n\\n    if sft_adapter:\\n        logger.info(\\\"Loading & merging SFT adapter: %s\\\",   \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    processor = DocREDProcessor(data_dir=data_dir)\\n    samples = processor.process(split)\\n    groups = processor.group_by_document(samples)\\n\\n    items = []\\n    for doc_id, D034 fix: Qwen3-4B generation_config.json overrides kwargs, must set explicitly\\n    model.generation_config.temperature = args.temperature\\n    model.generation_config.top_k = 0\\n    model.generation_config.top_p = args.top_p\\n    model.generation_config.do_sample = True\\n    if args.repetition_penalty != 1.0:\\n        model.generation_config.repetition_penalty = args.repetition_penalty\\n\\n    items = prepare_generation_data(args.data_path, args.split, tokenizer)\\n\\n    if args.num_shards > 1:\\n        total = len(items)\\n        per_shard = total // args.num_shards\\n        start = args.shard_id * per_shard\\n        end = start + per_shard if args.shard_id < args.num_shards - 1 else total\\n        items = items[start:end]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Shard %d/%d: docs [%d:%d] -> %d documents\\\",\\n                    args.shard_id, args.num_shards, start, end, len(items))\\n    elif args.end_idx > 0:\\n        items = items[args.start_idx:args.end_idx]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Slice: [%d:%d] -> %d documents\\\", args.start_idx, args.end_idx, len(items))\\n    elif args.start_idx > 0:\\n        items = items[args.start_idx:]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Slice: [%d:end] -> %d documents\\\", args.start_idx, len(items))\\n    else:\\n        logger.info(\\\"Full dataset: %d documents\\\", len(items))\\n\\n    completed = load_progress(output_dir)\\n    pending = [item for item in items if item[\\\"doc_id\\\"] not in completed]\\n    logger.info(\\\"Total: %d, completed: %d, pending: %d\\\", len(items), len(completed), len(pending))\\n\\n    if not pending:\\n        logger.info(\\\"All documents already generated. Done.\\\")\\n        return\\n\\n    # RSFT 监控计数器\\n    mon_format_ok = 0\\n    mon_truncated = 0\\n    mon_total_gens = 0\\n    mon_diversity_sum = 0.0\\n    mon_doc_count = 0\\n    mon_window_format_ok = 0\\n    mon_window_truncated = 0\\n    mon_window_total = 0\\n    mon_window_diversity = 0.0\\n    mon_window_docs = 0\\n\\n    def _print_monitor(prefix, n_docs, n_gens, n_fmt_ok, n_trunc, div_sum):\\n        fmt_rate = n_fmt_ok / n_gens * 100 if n_gens else 0\\n        trunc_rate = n_trunc / n_gens * 100 if n_gens else 0\\n        mean_div = div_sum / n_docs if n_docs else 0\\n        print(f\\\"\\\\n[RSFT Monitor] {prefix} ({n_docs} docs, {n_gens} generations):\\\")\\n        print(f\\\"  format_ok_rate: {fmt_rate:.1f}%\\\")\\n        print(f\\\"  truncation_rate: {trunc_rate:.1f}%\\\")\\n        n_gen = args.num_generations\\n        print(f\\\"  mean_diversity: {mean_div * n_gen:.1f}/{n_gen}\\\")\\n        if fmt_rate < 85:\\n            print(f\\\"  \\\\u26a0\\\\ufe0f WARNING: format_ok_rate below 85%, consider fixing parser\\\")\\n        if trunc_rate > 10:\\n            print(f\\\"  \\\\u26a0\\\\ufe0f WARNING: truncation_rate above 10%, consider max_new_tokens=3072\\\")\\n        print(flush=True)\\n\\n    # 追加写模式\\n    with open(output_file, \\\"a\\\") as fout:\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.31}}"}

  \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    processor = DocREDProcessor(data_dir=data_dir)\\n    samples = processor.process(split)\\n    groups = processor.group_by_document(samples)\\n\\n    items = []\\n    for doc_id, doc_samples in groups.items():\\n        sft_sample = processor.format_sft_sample(doc_samples)\\n        messages = [\\n            {\\\"role\\\": \\\"system\\\", \\\"content\\\": SYSTEM_PROMPT},\\n            {\\\"role\\\": \\\"user\\\", \\\"content\\\": f\\\"{sft_sample['instruction']}\\\\n\\\\n{sft_sample['input']}\\\"},\\n        ]\\n        prompt = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(\\n            messages, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True, enable_thinking=False,\\n        )\\n\\n        gold_triples = []\\n        for s in doc_samples:\\n            gold_triples.append({\\n                \\\"head\\\": s.head.name,\\n                \\\"relation\\\": s.relation_name,\\n                \\\"tail\\\": s.tail.name,\\n                \\\"evidence\\\": s.evidence_sent_ids,\\n                \\\"hard_negative_sent_ids\\\": s.hard_negative_sent_ids,\\n            })\\n\\n        ref = doc_samples\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 3880 字符] ...\\n\\non=\\\"store_true\\\",\\n                        help=\\\"4-bit 量化 (default: off)\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--start_idx\\\", type=int, default=0,\\n                        help=\\\"Start document index (inclusive)\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--end_idx\\\", type=int, default=-1,\\n                        help=\\\"End document index (exclusive), -1 means all\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--shard_id\\\", type=int, default=0,\\n                        help=\\\"Shard index for parallel generation\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--num_shards\\\", type=int, default=1,\\n                        help=\\\"Total number of shards for parallel generation\\\")\\n    parser.add_argument(\\\"--repetition_penalty\\\", type=float, default=1.0,\\n                        help=\\\"Repetition penalty (default: 1.0, no penalty)\\\")\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    torch.manual_seed(args.seed)\\n    if torch.cuda.is_available():\\n        torch.cuda.manual_seed_all(args.seed)\\n\\n    if args.num_shards > 1:\\n        output_dir = Path(args.output_dir) / f\\\"shard_{args.shard_id}\\\"\\n    else:\\n        output_dir = Path(args.output_dir)\\n    output_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\\n    output_file = output_dir / \\\"generations.jsonl\\\"\\n\\n    model, tokenizer = load_model_and_tokenizer(\\n        args.base_model, args.sft_adapter, quantize=args.quantize,\\n    )\\n\\n    # D034 fix: Qwen3-4B generation_config.json overrides kwargs, must set explicitly\\n    model.generation_config.temperature = args.temperature\\n    model.generation_config.top_k = 0\\n    model.generation_config.top_p = args.top_p\\n    model.generation_config.do_sample = True\\n    if args.repetition_penalty != 1.0:\\n        model.generation_config.repetition_penalty = args.repetition_penalty\\n\\n    items = prepare_generation_data(args.data_path, args.split, tokenizer)\\n\\n    if args.num_shards > 1:\\n        total = len(items)\\n        per_shard = total // args.num_shards\\n        start = args.shard_id * per_shard\\n        end = start + per_shard if args.shard_id < args.num_shards - 1 else total\\n        items = items[start:end]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Shard %d/%d: docs [%d:%d] -> %d documents\\\",\\n                    args.shard_id, args.num_shards, start, end, len(items))\\n    elif args.end_idx > 0:\\n        items = items[args.start_idx:args.end_idx]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Slice: [%d:%d] -> %d documents\\\", args.start_idx, args.end_idx, len(items))\\n    elif args.start_idx > 0:\\n        items = items[args.start_idx:]\\n        logger.info(\\\"Slice: [%d:end] -> %d documents\\\", args.start_idx, len(items))\\n    else:\\n        logger.info(\\\"Full dataset: %d documents\\\", len(items))\\n\\n    completed = load_progress(output_dir)\\n    pending = [item for item in items if item[\\\"doc_id\\\"] not in completed]\\n    logger.info(\\\"Total: %d, completed: %d, pending: %d\\\", len(items), len(completed), len(pending))\\n\\n    if not pending:\\n        logger.info(\\\"All documents already generated. ```python
def _print_monitor(prefix, n_docs, n_gens, n_fmt_ok, n_trunc, div_sum):

```

Completing the function...

The rest of the function calculates the mean diversity score and prints out a formatted monitor report showing the document count, generation count, format success rate, truncation rate, and normalized diversity score relative to the number of generations configured in args.

Looking at the math, `mean_div` is normalized to a 0-1 ratio by dividing the sum of diversity values by the document count, then multiplied back by `n_gen` to convert it to an absolute count. The diversity calculation itself—unique triple sets per generation—appears sound, so the original monitoring bug that showed ~1/8 instead of ~7/8 must have been elsewhere in the pipeline rather than in this formula.

Comparing the old and new code, both use `args.num_generations` for the denominator and format the output the same way, so the discrepancy likely came from something like a scoping issue or the generation config producing identical outputs during the initial monitoring run, even though the full-file cross-check revealed the actual diversity was much higher. The generation config fix ensures the temperature setting actually applies, and the independent file-based cross-check provides a safety net—even if the inline monitoring has lingering issues, the final verification will catch them. The diversity module itself is well-structured and testable, with proper edge case handling, though the inline formula may still show misleading intermediate results if the original bug was there.

However, the running processes started before the fix was deployed, so they're still using the old code—the new cross-check won't execute until those processes restart or the generation completes and restarts.
````
